[ˆ\"\\] denotes every character except\n\" and \\. Down: Two possible matching stacks for matching the\nstring [\"a to the CFG. Each stack represents a possible expansion\nof the rules in the CFG. The edges and nodes in the stack corre-\nspond to the transitions and states in the PDA in Figure 1.\napplied in LLM structured generation.\nPushdown automata (PDA) (Sch¨utzenberger, 1963; Evey,\n1963) are typically used to recognize languages generated by\nCFGs, as they employ a stack to manage nested structures.\nIn this paper, we use a definition of PDA that is equivalent\nto the original one, but more conducive to explaining the\nalgorithm. An example of PDA is shown in Figure 1, and\nits stacks are shown in detail in Figure 3. A PDA consists\nof multiple finite state automata (FSA), each representing\na grammar rule, with the stack handling recursive rule ex-\npansions. The transitions in the FSA include two types:\ncharacter edges, which accept specific characters, and rule\nreference edges, which allow recursive entry into other rules.\nA formal definition of the PDA is provided in Appendix A.\nTo match a string, the PDA begins with the main rule, recur-\nsively expanding child rules by pushing rule-reference edges\nonto the stack; once a rule is fully matched, it pops the stack\nto return to the previous rule. The top of the stack holds the\ncurrent node reached. If the grammar is non-deterministic,\nmeaning there can be multiple possible transitions in the\n\n\n--- PAGE 4 ---\nXGrammar: Flexible and Efficient Structured Generation Engine For Large Language Models\nab\"\nm\nh\n[8] '\"'\nstr:\n[9] [^\"\\]\nl\n[10] '\"'\nContext-dependent\na\n\\\n\\ab\n\"ab\na\"b\nReaches l or m from l\nCannot match from l\nReaches m from l but\nhave remaining part\nContext-independent\n(Accepted)\nContext-independent\n(Rejected)\nVocabulary\nFigure 4. An example for the token mask cache. Tokens are cate-\ngorized into three types: context-independent (accepted), context-\nindependent (rejected), and context-dependent. The first two types\ncan be directly determined for mask generation at runtime.\nPDA for the same input character, the PDA can maintain\nmultiple parallel stacks for each path, ensuring flexibility.\nHowever, the unbounded stack length results in an infinite\nnumber of possible states, making it impractical to precom-\npute token masks for all scenarios, thus posing challenges\nfor efficient constrained decoding.\n3\nXGRAMMAR\nAs shown in Figure 1, XGrammar utilizes a byte-level push-\ndown automaton to interpret the context-free grammar. This\nbyte-level design allows each character edge to include one\nor more bytes, handling irregular token boundaries and sup-\nporting tokens containing sub-UTF8 characters. The au-\ntomaton’s structure is optimized to accelerate matching,\nas described in §3.4. In the preprocessing phase, we gen-\nerate an adaptive token mask cache, as detailed in §3.1,\nwhich accelerates runtime mask generation by precomput-\ning context-independent tokens. The effectiveness of this\ncache is further enhanced by context extension in §3.2. At\nruntime, the token mask cache quickly generates most of the\nmask, while the persistent execution stack in §3.3 efficiently\nprocesses the rest context-dependent tokens. Additionally,\nmask generation and LLM inference are overlapped in §3.5\nto minimize the overhead of constrained decoding. Once the\nLLM generates a new token under the mask constraint, this\ntoken is then used to update the stack state of the pushdown\nautomaton for the next mask generation.\n3.1\nAdaptive Token Mask Cache\nTo accelerate the generation of the token mask cache, the\nadaptive token cache categorizes tokens into two types\n(Figure 4): context-independent tokens, which constitute\nthe vast majority and can be pre-computed, and context-\ndependent tokens, which require slower, on-the-fly process-\ning but are relatively few. This token classification relates to\nhow tokens are validated by the pushdown automaton. We\nfound that, considering the transition of the stack state, the\nprocess of matching tokens to the automaton can be divided\ninto three categories:\n1. The matching process expands into a child rule, push-\ning new elements onto the stack.\n2. The matching process advances within the current rule,\nupdating the stack top node to a new position.\n3. The matching process reaches the end of the current\nrule and returns to a parent rule, popping elements\nfrom the stack.\nValidating tokens in the former two cases only relies on the\nstack top node, which represents the position within the cur-\nrent rule, so we define these tokens as context-independent\ntokens. The tokens in the third type, however, requires\ninspecting the entire running stack in validation, and are\ndefined as context-dependent tokens. For every node of the\npushdown automaton, there is a set of context-independent\ntokens with this node being at the top of the stack at run-\ntime, and their validity can be determined ahead of time.\nTherefore, we precompute the validity of these tokens and\nstore them in a cache with the stack top node as the key,\nwhich we refer to as the adaptive token mask cache. It also\nadaptively selects the most efficient storage format based on\nthe cache’s contents, as explained in the next paragraph.\nAt runtime, we retrieve the validity of context-independent\ntokens directly based on the top of the stack to generate the\ntoken mask. The remaining few context-dependent tokens\nare validated by executing the pushdown automaton with\nthe full stack. If parallel stacks exist due to the ambiguity\nof the grammar, the token masks for every stack is merged\ninto a final token mask by finding the union of the accepted\ntokens in each mask. The computation for the token mask\nis significantly reduced because our method do not need\nto check context-independent tokens at runtime. Experi-\nments show that context-dependent tokens account for only\na minor proportion, amounting to less than 1% (1134 out of\n128k) for the Llama-3.1 model using JSON grammar.\nAdaptive storage.\nThe token mask cache adopts an adap-\ntive storage format to reduce memory usage, as illustrated\nin Figure 5. For each automaton node, the token mask cache\ndivides the vocabulary into three parts: the accepted context-\nindependent tokens, the rejected context-independent to-\nkens, and the context-dependent tokens. Since these three\nparts together cover all tokens, it is sufficient to store only\nthe two smaller subsets. We observe that, for a set of context-\nindependent tokens, they tend to be either almost entirely\naccepted, namely accept-heavy cases, or almost entirely\n\n\n--- PAGE 5 ---\nXGrammar: Flexible and Efficient Structured Generation Engine For Large Language Models\nAccept-heavy Cases: Store \nReject-heavy Cases: Store \nEqual Cases: Very rare, store bitsets\nFigure 5. The adaptive storage format. In accept-heavy cases, we\nstore the rejected tokens and context-dependent tokens. In reject-\nheavy cases, we store the accepted tokens and context-dependent\ntokens. In rare cases where two kinds of tokens are equal, we\ncompress the accepted and rejected tokens into a bitset of the\nvocabulary size.\nrejected, namely reject-heavy cases. This arises because, if\nwildcards can be matched from the current node, such as the\nwildcard [ˆ\"\\]* in the rule of string, nearly all tokens are\nvalid; whereas if the node only accepts a few specific charac-\nters, nearly all tokens are invalid. Based on this observation,\nwe designed the following adaptive storage format:\n1. For accept-heavy cases, we store the rejected context-\nindependent tokens and context-dependent tokens in\ntwo arrays.\n2. For reject-heavy cases, we store the accepted context-\nindependent tokens and context-dependent tokens in\ntwo arrays.\n3. For rare cases where the accepted and rejected tokens\nare roughly equal, we store the accepted and rejected\ncontext-independent tokens and compress them into a\nbitset matching the vocabulary size.\nThus, in both accept-heavy and reject-heavy cases, the adap-\ntive storage format only requires storing a small subset of\ntokens, significantly reducing memory usage. In practice,\nwe will enumerate the three storage types, calculate their re-\nspective costs, and choose the storage type with the smallest\nsize. For Llama-3.1 model and JSON grammar, this adap-\ntive storage method can effectively reduce the total memory\nusage to 0.2% (from 160 MB to 0.46 MB).\nAdditionally, when multiple parallel stacks exists, we need\nto merge the token masks. The merging algorithm of token\nmasks is optimized based on storage type, as shown in Algo-\nrithm 1. For an accept-heavy mask (many accepted tokens,\nstoring only rejected tokens), it intersects the rejected tokens\nwith PartialRej. For a reject-heavy mask (many rejected\ntokens, storing only accepted tokens), it combines accepted\ntokens with PartialAcc. In the final mask, the rejected to-\nkens are the set difference PartialRej \\ PartialAcc. This\nalgorithm limits set operations to small token subsets, thus\nenhancing efficiency.\nAlgorithm 1 Efficiently Merge Token Masks\nInput:\nToken masks for k parallel stacks {Mi\n=\n(Acci, Reji)}k\ni=1, vocabulary V.\nOutput: The final token mask M = (Acc, Rej).\nInitialize PartialAcc ←∅, PartialRej ←V\nfor i = 1 to k do\nif Mi is accept-heavy then\nMi only stores rejected token list Reji\nPartialRej ←PartialRej ∩Reji\nelse\nMi only stores accepted token list Acci\nPartialAcc ←PartialAcc ∪Acci\nend if\nend for\nM ←(V \\ (PartialRej \\ PartialAcc),\nPartialRej \\ PartialAcc)\n3.2\nContext Expansion\nAlthough the adaptive token mask cache effectively re-\nduces the number of tokens checked at runtime, checking\nall context-dependent tokens remains an efficiency bottle-\nneck at runtime. To further reduce the number of context-\ndependent tokens, XGrammar introduces context expansion,\nwhich leverages the grammar’s context information to reject\nmore context-dependent tokens during preprocessing, as\nshown in Figure 6.\nAs described in the last section, a token is context-dependent\nwhen we reach the end of the current rule during matching,\nbut there is still a remaining part of the token that requires\nfurther checking by returning to the parent rules. However,\nthrough analysis of the grammar, we can observe that in a\nlarge portion of the cases, the remaining part of a token is\ninvalid. This is because, for many rules, when they reach\ntheir end, there are only a limited number of positions they\ncan return to within their parent rules, and the set of strings\nthat can be further matched from those positions is also\nlimited.\nBased on this observation, context expansion precomputes,\nfor each rule, the set of strings that can be accepted after\nreturning to the parent rules, called the expanded suffix.\nThe remaining unmatched part of the context-dependent\ntokens should either be a prefix of the expanded suffix or\nstart with the expanded suffix. Otherwise, they are rejected.\n\n\n--- PAGE 6 ---\nXGrammar: Flexible and Efficient Structured Generation Engine For Large Language Models\n[8] '\"'\n[9] [^\"\\]\n[10] '\"'\nm\nh\nstr:\nl\nExpanded Suffix\nfor str\nBefore Context Expansion\n,\nAfter Context Expansion\nContext-dependent\n\"ab\na\"b\n\",\n\"]\nContext-dependent\n\",\n\"]\nContext-independent\n(Rejected)\n\"ab\na\"b\nRejected by the\nexpanded suffix\n]\nFigure 6. The context expansion. Each rule obtains a set of ex-\npanded suffices, representing the set of strings that must be\nmatched after completing this rule. The remaining unmatched\npart of the context-dependent tokens should either be a prefix of\nthe expanded suffix or start with the expanded suffix. Otherwise,\nthey are rejected.\nThis filtering process effectively reduces the number of\ncontext-dependent tokens by eliminating those that would\nfail in higher-level rule contexts. Applied to the Llama-3.1\nmodel and JSON grammar, this technique reduces context-\ndependent tokens by 90% (from 1,134 to 120).\nAlgorithm 2 describes the context expansion process that\nfinds the expanded suffix of each rule. For a rule R, we\nutilize a finite state automaton (FSA) Actx\nR (ctx is the abbre-\nviation for context) to represent the expanded suffix, and that\nis extracted from the pushdown automata. We first find all\nedges e = (s, t) in the pushdown automata that references\nR and belongs to rule R′. R′ is not necessarily different\nfrom R. Then we find a subgraph of the automaton of rule\nR′ starting from t to represent the possible strings that can\nfollow R via depth-first search (DFS). However, we will not\nconsider edges in the subgraph that reference other rules\nto avoid recursive references between rules, so the edges\nin the extracted subgraph will only have character labels.\nAlgorithm 2 Extract the Expanded Suffix Automaton\nInput: Pushdown automaton P, rule R\nOutput: Expanded context FSA Actx\nR for R\nInitialize Actx\nR as an empty FSA\nfor edge s\nR\n−→t in P referencing R do\n{ Aδ is an FSA for the partial result }\nInitialize Aδ as an empty FSA, visited ←{}\nAdd node t to Aδ\nEXTRACTONE(t, Aδ, visited)\n{ Merge the partial result into the final result }\nActx\nR ←FSAUNION(Actx\nR , Aδ)\nend for\nfunction EXTRACTONE(start, Aδ, visited)\nif start in visited then\nreturn\nend if\nAdd start to visited\n{ Stop search for nodes with rule-referencing edges }\nif start is a final node in P or\nhas an edge referencing another rule then\nMark start as final in Aδ\nreturn\nend if\n{Now all outward edges of start are character edges}\nfor edge start\nc−→end from start do\nAdd end and start\nc−→end to Aδ\nEXTRACTONE(end, Aδ, visited)\nend for\nend function\nIf a node has both character edges and edges referencing\nother rules, we will stop the search at this node. The ex-\ntracted subgraph is then merged into Actx\nR . This process is\nrepeated for all rules, and the extracted Actx\nR is used to reject\ncontext-dependent tokens cannot match any string in it after\nfinishing matching rule R.\nAlthough we do not consider rule-referencing edges when\nextracting the expanded context automata, this algorithm\ncan still extract many useful context information. That is\nbecause the inlining optimization introduced in §3.4 inlines\nfragment rules into their parent rules, reducing the need to\ncheck into child rules to reject context-dependent tokens.\n3.3\nPersistent Execution Stack\nAs the grammar engine still needs to handle context-\ndependent tokens, we need to efficiently execute the push-\ndown automata for these tokens. Additionally, we also need\nto execute the pushdown automata for preprocessing the\ncontext-independent token sets for all positions in the push-\ndown automata. In both cases, we need to maintain multiple\n\n\n--- PAGE 7 ---\nXGrammar: Flexible and Efficient Structured Generation Engine For Large Language Models\n[0] array\n[3] str\nNode l\n[5] str\nNode l\nNode d\n[0]<array>\n[3]<str>\nNode l\n[0]<array>\n[5]<str>\nNode l\nStacks of \nthe current step\nStacks from\nprevious steps\n[0]<array>\nNode d\nNode a\nRoot\nNode a\nPersistent\nExecution\nStack\nFigure 7. The persistent stack organizes multiple matching stacks\nfrom the current step, as well as stacks from previous steps, into a\nsingle tree. It reduces memory consumption and supports rolling\nthe state back to previous steps.\nparallel stacks and branch out as we match the characters\nin each token. To support efficient state branching, we in-\ntroduce the persistent execution stack (Driscoll et al., 1989)\nto manage the multiple stacks and efficiently execute the\npushdown automata. It can also manage the stacks from\nprevious time points and enable the state rollback opera-\ntion, effectively speeding up the execution of the pushdown\nautomata on a set of tokens.\nAs shown in Figure 7, the persistent execution stack man-\nages a set of stacks, which are either the parallel stacks\nfrom the current time point or the stacks from previous time\npoints, into a single tree, and every stack is represented by a\npath from the root node on the tree. The stack top node is\nstored as a pointer to the node in the tree. Since the stacks\nfrom adjacent time points often share most of the deeper\nelements and only a few nodes are pushed or popped, this\nmerging avoids memory redundancy for storing multiple\nstacks. When matching a new character from a token, we\nmay need to split the stack into multiple stacks due to the\nambiguity of the grammar, each corresponding to a different\nexpansion of grammar rules. In this case, we only need to\nsplit the branch for that stack instead of copying the whole\nstack, which reduces the overhead of state branching.\nAdditionally, the persistent execution stack enables fast state\nrollback by maintaining the stack from previous time points.\nAt runtime, a sliding window of history is maintained. To\nroll back to a previous state, we only need to change the\ncurrent stack pointers, which requires constant time. This\nrollback operation is particularly useful for checking a large\nset of tokens, as many tokens share a common prefix with\nother tokens, such as read, ready, and reader all sharing\nthe prefix read. All the checked tokens are sorted in lexico-\ngraphical order to find the maximum length of the common\nprefixes. Then the tokens are checked one by one, and be-\nfore checking each token, the state rolls back to just after\nthe common prefix with the previous token. Therefore, we\ncan avoid the redundant checks of these common prefixes,\nreducing the number of characters that need to be checked.\nFor Llama-3.1 model and JSON grammar, this approach\nreduces the number of characters that need to be checked\nacross the entire vocabulary to 30%, significantly speeding\nup the preprocessing stage.\nThe rollback operation enables more applications with\nefficient structured generation.\nThere are many LLM ap-\nplications that involve rolling back the output to a previous\ntoken. For instance, the jump-forward decoding (Yin et al.,\n2024) requires retokenization, which involves rolling back\nsome tokens in the context and then inserting new tokens. To\nensure structured generation can continue after rolling back\ntokens, we can roll back the automaton state simultaneously\nwith the output token rollback. There are also many LLM\napplications that requires LLMs generate in a tree struc-\nture, such as in Tree-of-thought (Yao et al., 2024), SGLang\n(Zheng et al., 2024), and the speculative model in the spec-\nulative decoding algorithm SpecInfer (Miao et al., 2024).\nWe can maintain the automata state for every branch of the\noutput tree, and when the output branches, we can quickly\nsplit the automaton state, maintaining separate matching\nstates for each output branch. This branching is fast because\nwe only need to maintain the stack top pointer on the tree\nfor every branch. Therefore, the persistent execution stack\nenables us to ensure efficient structured generation for all\nthese applications.\n3.4\nPushdown Automata Structure Optimizations\nWe will perform additional optimizations to improve the\nstructure of pushdown automata to speed up the efficiency\nof final execution. These optimizations draw from tradi-\ntional compiler optimization concepts, but we find them\nparticularly useful for efficient constrained decoding.\nRule inlining.\nThere could be many fragment rules, i.e.\nrules with only a few elements, in the specified context-free\ngrammar, which are then converted into small FSA in the\npushdown automaton. On the one hand, this increases the\nambiguity of the grammar since we need to inspect into\nthese fragment rules and check during the execution of the\npushdown automata. On the other hands, during context\nexpansion, references to fragment rules are not considered,\n\n\n--- PAGE 8 ---\nXGrammar: Flexible and Efficient Structured Generation Engine For Large Language Models\nso the extracted context automata will be smaller. We will\nmiss the opportunity to reject context-dependent tokens\nbased on the structure of these fragment rules.\nTo address this issue, we introduce an automatic inlining\nstrategy (Scheifler, 1977) for fragment rules. We iteratively\npick rules that do not reference other rules and inline them\ninto the parent rules. To avoid the explosion of the automa-\nton size, we limit the size of the inlined rule and the size\nof inlined result to constants. This inlining process almost\neliminated fragment rules, thereby improving the efficiency\nof token checking and enhancing the effectiveness of the\ncontext expansion.\nPushdown automata node merging.\nFor pushdown au-\ntomata, in many cases, the ambiguity comes from multiple\noutward edges of a node with the same label. When match-\ning tokens, if we arrive at this node, and the next character\njust matches the label, the matching stack will be split into\nmultiple stacks, one for each outward edge. The increase in\nthe number of stacks increases the computation as we need\nto check the context-dependent tokens for each stack and\nmerge the token masks. To reduce this kind of ambiguity,\nthe node merging algorithm merges the subsequent nodes\nthat satisfy: a) they are pointed to by edges with the same\nlabel originating from the same point b) they are not pointed\nto by other edges.\nAdditionally, the epsilon edge also increases the ambigu-\nity of the matching process. An epsilon edge s\nϵ−→t in\nthe automata means that the matching process can directly\nmove from s to t without consuming any characters. If the\nmatching process arrives at s, the execution stack will split\ninto two stacks, one with s at the top and the other with t,\nboth of which can continue matching. To reduce this kind\nof ambiguity, the node merging algorithm also merges the\nnodes s and t into a single node, as long as s has no other\noutward edge or t has no zero inward edge.\nThese two optimizations preserves the equivalence of the\nautomaton, but reduces the number of nodes and edges. At\nruntime, the number of stacks and the computation required\nfor token checking are reduced, speeding up the mask gen-\neration process.\n3.5\nOverlapping Mask Generation and LLM\nInference\nWith the optimizations mentioned above, the token mask\ngeneration process is significantly accelerated, but it still\nrequires CPU computation. To further eliminate the over-\nhead of constrained decoding, we overlap the computation\nfor mask generation with the LLM inference process, as\nshown in Figure 8. We observed that the mask generation\nprocess and LLM inference process can be overlapped. That\nis because the mask generation only requires CPU, and only\nLLM Prefilling\nBuild Mask Cache\nCPU\nGPU\nMask\nGen\nSampling\nLLM Decoding\nMask\nGen\nSampling\nSync\nSync\nFigure 8. Overlapping building the mask cache with LLM prefill-\ning, and mask generation with LLM decoding to minimize the\noverhead.\ndepends on the previously generated tokens. The LLM infer-\nence process except the sampling stage only requires GPU,\nand also only depends on the previously generated tokens.\nTherefore, we can parallelize the mask generation process\non the CPU with the LLM inference process on the GPU.\nWe will synchronize before sampling, and the GPU will ob-\ntain the mask from the CPU and perform masked sampling\nto generate the new token. Additionally, the preprocessing\nstage can also be overlapped with the LLM prefilling stage,\nwhere the LLM processes the prompt. This orchestration\nbetween CPU and GPU ensures that the token restrictions\nare applied seamlessly, with almost zero overhead for LLM\ninference. In practice, the time for mask generation is less\nthan the time for LLM inference, so the mask generation\nprocess will not become the bottleneck of the generation\nprocess.\n4\nEVALUATION\nWe implement XGrammar in 12,000 lines of core C++ code,\nand we provide Python bindings to facilitate seamless inte-\ngration with LLM inference frameworks. In this section, we\nevaluate XGrammar to answer the following questions:\n• Can XGrammar efficiently support each step of con-\nstrained decoding? (§4.1)\n• Does XGrammar achieve minimal overhead for end-to-\nend structured generation in LLM serving? (§4.2)\n• How effective is each optimization technique intro-\nduced in XGrammar? (§4.3)\n• How does XGrammar effect downstream structured\ngeneration tasks? (§4.4)\n4.1\nMask generation efficiency\nThis section evaluates the efficiency of mask generation to\nmeasure the overhead introduced by constraint decoding.\nWe first assess regex-based methods using JSON schemas,\nwhich can be converted into regex. To test more complex\ncases beyond regex capabilities, we evaluate context-free\ngrammars, including unconstrained JSON (from ECMA-\n404 (Ecma International, 2013)), XML (based on the XML\n\n\n--- PAGE 9 ---\nXGrammar: Flexible and Efficient Structured Generation Engine For Large Language Models\n102\n103\n104\nTime (us/token)\n36\n125\n7069\n6147\nJSON Schema\n102\n103\n104\nTime (us/token)\n36\n4711\n9353\nCFG (Unconstrained JSON)\n102\n103\n104\n105\n106\nTime (us/token)\n52\n382126\n18231\nCFG (XML)\n102\n103\n104\n105\n106\nTime (us/token)\n191\n427285\n42577\nCFG (Python DSL)\nXGrammar\nOutlines\nllama.cpp-Grammar\nlm-format-enforcer\nFigure 9. Unconstrained JSON cannot be han-\ndled by regex-based methods due to its support for arbitrar-\nily nested lists and objects. The Python DSL covers basic\ncontrol flow (if, for, while) and data types (str, int, float,\nbool) but ignores indentation. For JSON schema and uncon-\nstrained JSON, we use the JSON-mode-eval dataset (Nous-\nResearch, 2024), and for XML and Python, we use a syn-\nthetic dataset. For baselines, we choose three popular con-\nstrained generation libraries: Outlines (Willard & Louf,\n2023) (v1.0), the grammar engine in llama.cpp (Gerganov,\n2023) (b3998), and lm-format-enforcer (Gat et al., 2025)\n(v0.10.9, a regex-based method that does not support CFG).\nAll methods are evaluated on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct using\nan AMD Ryzen 9 7950X CPU and an NVIDIA RTX 4090\nGPU.\nThe results are shown in Figure 9. XGrammar consistently\nachieves the lowest latency across all tasks, with under\n40 µs per token for JSON Schema and CFG (JSON), and\nunder 200 µs for XML and Python DSL. It delivers up to 3x\nspeedup on JSON Schema and over 100x on CFG, compared\nto the best baseline in each case.\n4.2\nEnd-to-End LLM Engine Evaluation\nThis section evaluates XGrammar in LLM serving scenarios.\nWe co-design XGrammar with the serving engines using the\noverlapping technique introduced in §3.5. We integrate it\ninto widely used end-to-end LLM serving engines, including\nthe C++-based MLC-LLM (MLC team, 2023a) and the\nPython-based SGLang (Zheng et al., 2024), showcasing\nTable 1. We begin\nby implementing a baseline using a pushdown automaton\nparser without any optimizations, where each token mask is\ngenerated by checking the entire vocabulary to determine\nwhether parsing can proceed. Building on this baseline, we\nprogressively add the optimizations described in this paper,\nnamely node merging, the adaptive token mask cache, rule\nTable 4. Impact of XGrammar on structured generation tasks.\nXGrammar ensures 100% syntactic correctness of the generated\noutputs.\nTask\nAccuracy w/o\nAccuracy w/\nXGrammar\nXGrammar\nFunction calling\n62%\n100%\nXML code generation\n80%\n100%\ninlining, and context expansion. For each configuration, we\nmeasure the average mask generation time on the CFG (un-\nconstrained JSON) task with the Llama-3.1 8B model and\nthe json-mode-eval dataset. As shown in Table 3, the results\nshow that the adaptive token mask cache has the greatest\nimpact in terms of speedup, while other techniques, includ-\ning node merging, rule inlining, and context expansion, also\nyield noticeable improvements.\n4.4\nImpact of XGrammar on Structured Generation\nTasks\nXGrammar can improve the generation quality of LLMs by\nensuring that the output strictly adheres to the given format.\nWe evaluate the impact of XGrammar on two strcutured\ngeneration tasks: function calling (i.e., JSON generation\nguided by a JSON schema) and XML code generation. For\nfunction calling, we use the json-mode-eval dataset, while\nfor XML code generation, we rely on a synthetic dataset.\nWe measure the syntactic correctness of the generated func-\ntion calling outputs and XML code using LLaMA-3.1 8B.\nAs shown in Table 4, XGrammar significantly improves\ngeneration accuracy. We observe that without XGrammar,\nthe model often includes additional explanations alongside\nthe intended code output, or the generated JSON contains\nan unexpected type. This makes the output unsuitable for\ndirect use by downstream applications. XGrammar avoids\nthis issue by enforcing grammar constraints.\n\n\n--- PAGE 11 ---\nXGrammar: Flexible and Efficient Structured Generation Engine For Large Language Models\n5\nRELATED WORK\nSeveral works looked at algorithm improvements for struc-\ntured generation. (Koo et al., 2024) proposes an algorithm\nto convert character-level pushdown automata to token-level\npushdown automata. (Wang et al., 2023) specifies LLM\noutput structure through prompting. (Rozi`ere et al., 2024;\nChaudhary, 2023; Li et al., 2023) explore finetuning LLMs\nfor higher quality structured generation. XGrammar’s ap-\nproach is orthogonal to these methods and can be combined\nwith these approaches.\nThere has also been some previous work focusing on con-\nstrained decoding. Some methods use regex to represent\nsyntax, such as lm-format-enforcer (Gat, 2024), but they\ncannot handle more complex CFGs. Synchromesh (Poesia\net al., 2022) and llama.cpp (Gerganov, 2023) utilizes LR\nparser and PDA respectively to handle CFG and generate\nthe mask, but requires checking the entire vocabulary at\nruntime, which incurs large overhead. Outlines (Willard\n& Louf, 2023) handles the grammar through a lexer and\nparser, and uses caching to accelerate mask generation, but\nit only considers the most recent lexer token, which may\nlead to incorrect judgments when LLM tokens span across\nmultiple lexer tokens (Appendix A in (Koo et al., 2024)).\nSyncode (Ugare et al., 2024) uses a lexer and parser with a\ncache spanning multiple lexer tokens, but requires all tokens\nto be processed offline, leading to substantial preprocessing\noverhead. In this work, XGrammar leverages a PDA to sup-\nport CFGs and introduces an adaptive token mask cache to\nadaptively handle tokens during preprocessing and runtime,\ncombining other system optimizations to achieve minimal\noverhead in both stages.\nGuidance (Guidance-ai, 2024), LMQL (Beurer-Kellner,\n2023), SGLang (Zheng et al., 2024) provide flexible ways\nto declare the structures. XGrammar is complementary to\nthese improvements and can be used as the backend engine\nto speedup their execution.\nLLM serving engines (MLC team, 2023a; Zheng et al.,\n2024; Kwon et al., 2023a; hiworldwzj et al., 2024) em-\nploy various techniques to support efficient LLM generation\nfor multiple concurrent users, including engine-level tech-\nniques such as continuous batching (Yu et al., 2022) for\ndyanmic request scheduling, and low-level KV cache tech-\nnique PagedKVCache (Kwon et al., 2023a) for efficient\nmemory management. Also, AICI (Moskal et al., 2024)\nproposes a CPU-GPU parallel computation paradigm to ac-\ncelerate structured generation. These LLM serving engines\ncan leverage XGrammar for efficient, structured generation\non top of their existing LLM inference techniques.\n6\nCONCLUSION\nWe proposed XGrammar, a flexible and efficient structured\ngeneration engine for LLMs. XGrammar separates the\nvocabulary into context-independent tokens and context-\ndependent ones. It prechecks the context-dependent tokens\nand stores the result in an adaptive token mask cache. We\nfurther introduce a persistent stack to speed up the execu-\ntion of context-dependent checks. Finally, we co-design\nthe grammar engine with LLM inference to overlap gram-\nmar execution with GPU computation. Our system greatly\nspeeds up the token mask generation process in token mask\nand enables zero overhead structure generation in end-to-\nend LLM inference flows. We hope our system can enable\na broader range of structure generation across platforms.\nACKNOWLEDGEMENTS\nThis work was supported in part by NSF award CNS-\n2211882, and gifts from OctoAI, Qualcomm, and CMU\nopensource software fellowships. E., Bar-\nrett, C., and Sheng, Y.\nSglang: Efficient execution\nof structured language model programs, 2024.\nURL\nhttps://arxiv.org/abs/2312.07104.\nA\nFORMAL DEFINITION OF THE PDA\nVARIANT\nIn our paper, we define a variant of the pushdown automaton\n(PDA) that is equivalent to the original definition, but is\ndesigned to facilitate the description of the parsing algorithm\nand the construction of the token mask cache, since the keys\nof the token mask cache are precisely the states in this PDA.\nIt is defined as the tuple\nP =\n\u0000R, Σ, {Ar}r∈R, qmain, δ\n\u0001\n,\nwhere:\n• R is a finite set of grammar rules.\n• Σ is a finite input alphabet.\n• For each rule r ∈R, the corresponding finite state\nautomaton is given by\nAr =\n\u0000Qr, Σ ∪R, qstart\nr\n, Fr, δr\n\u0001\n,\nwhere Qr is a finite set of states, qstart\nr\n∈Qr is the start\nstate, Fr ⊆Qr is the set of accepting states, and δr is\nthe transition function defined over Qr. The transition\nlabels in Ar are drawn from the alphabet Σ ∪R, which\nincludes both input characters and rule references.\n• qmain is the start state corresponding to the main rule.\n• δ is the global transition function that governs the oper-\nation of the PDA by handling two kinds of transitions:\n– Character transitions: When in a state q ∈Qr,\nreading an input symbol a ∈Σ may lead to a\ntransition within the same automaton, i.e., q\na−→\nq′.\n– Rule reference transitions: When in a state q ∈\nQr, a transition labeled by a rule reference to\ns ∈R allows the PDA to push the current return\ninformation onto the stack and jump to the start\nstate qstart\ns\nof As.\nThe parsing state is represented by a set of pairs {(si, qi)},\nwhere si denotes the content of the stack (encoding return\ninformation), qi is the current state (with qi ∈Qr for some\nr ∈R). There can be multiple such pairs because the push-\ndown automaton can contain non-deterministic transitions,\nwhich means there could be multiple possible parsing stacks\nand states. In the main body of the paper, for simplicity, we\nplace the current state qi at the top of the stack. Thus, the\nparsing state is represented as a set of parsing stacks.\nWe now describe a formal transformation that converts the\nabove variant PDA definition into a standard PDA. To obtain\na standard PDA, we construct a new pushdown automaton\nP ′ =\n\u0000Q, Σ, Γ, δ′, q0, F\n\u0001\nas follows. The state set Q is defined as the union of all\nstates from the FSAs:\nQ =\n[\nr∈R\nQr,\nand we set the initial state to be q0 = qmain. The stack\nalphabet Γ is chosen to record return information; we define\nΓ = { (r, q) | r ∈R, q ∈Qr },\nso that each symbol (r, q) encodes the context of a rule call,\nnamely the originating rule and the return state.\nThe transition function δ′ of the standard PDA is then de-\nfined to simulate the behavior of the variant PDA. For each\ncharacter transition in some δr, if\nq\na−→q′\nwith a ∈Σ and q, q′ ∈Qr,\nwe include in δ′ the transition\nδ′(q, a, γ) ∋(q′, γ)\nfor all γ ∈Γ.\nFor each rule reference transition in δr, if\nq\ns−→q′\nwith s ∈R,\nwe simulate the recursive call by defining an ϵ-transition\nthat pushes the return information and transfers control to\nthe called rule. Formally, we set\nδ′(q, ϵ, γ) ∋\n\u0010\nqstart\ns\n, (r, q′) · γ\n\u0011\nfor all γ ∈Γ,\nwhere q belongs to the automaton Ar, and (r, q′) · γ denotes\nthe stack obtained by pushing (r, q′) onto γ. Finally, when\n\n\n--- PAGE 17 ---\nXGrammar: Flexible and Efficient Structured Generation Engine For Large Language Models\nan automaton Ar reaches an accepting state q ∈Fr, the\nstandard PDA simulates the return from a recursive call by\npopping the top of the stack. That is, if the current stack has\nthe form (r′, q′) · γ, we define\nδ′(q, ϵ, (r′, q′)) ∋(q′, γ).\nThe set of accepting states F is defined as those states in\nS\nr∈R Fr that are reached with an empty stack.\nThis construction shows that character transitions in the\nvariant PDA directly correspond to state transitions with-\nout stack operations in P ′, while rule reference transitions\ncorrespond to stack push and jump operations. Similarly,\ncompleting the match of an FSA and returning to the par-\nent rule is achieved by a stack pop and a transition to the\nstored return state. In this way, the standard PDA P ′ exactly\nsimulates the recursive behavior of our variant PDA.\nB\nSYNERGY BETWEEN XGRAMMAR AND\nJUMP-FORWARD DECODING\nJump-forward decoding is a technique designed to acceler-\nate structured generation. When, based on the current input,\nthe following output can be deterministically inferred from\nthe grammar, it bypasses LLM decoding and sampling by\ndirectly tokenizing and appending the output to the context.\nThis improves the end-to-end efficiency of LLM generation.\nThis technique is orthogonal to the constrained decoding\nadopted by XGrammar. XGrammar further supports jump-\nforward decoding and demonstrates that the two can be\neffectively combined to further improve efficiency.\nWe evaluate performance using the Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct\nmodel served through the SGLang engine, running on a ma-\nchine with an AMD Ryzen 9 7950X CPU and an NVIDIA\nRTX 4090 GPU. All experiments are conducted with a batch\nsize of 1. We compare our method, XGrammar, against the\nexisting decoding backend Outlines, testing both with and\nwithout jump-forward decoding enabled. We measure time\nper output token as the evaluation metric.\nAs shown in Figure 11, XGrammar consistently outperforms\nOutlines, and achieves the best efficiency when combined\nwith jump-forward decoding—demonstrating its ability to\nbetter leverage structural constraints for faster generation.\nC\nCROSS-PLATFORM DEPLOYMENT OF\nXGRAMMAR\nWe study bringing XGrammar to a wide variety of plat-\nforms. We leverage Emscripten (Zakai, 2011) to compile\nXGrammar into WebAssembly (Haas et al., 2017) and build\na JavaScript binding. This approach enables XGrammar to\nrun in client-side browsers on portable devices like laptops\nand mobile phones. We further integrate the web-binding\nw/o\njump-forward\nw/\njump-forward\n0\n20\n40\nTime per output token (ms)\n44.2\n6.8\n31.5\n5.4\nOutlines\nXGrammar\nFigure 11. The results show that XGrammar\nbrings close to zero overhead in both settings, enabling a\ngreat potential to support future on-device agents with high\nperformance.\n", "excerpt_ranges": [[0, 78203]], "range_unit": "zero-based half-open characters in extracted text", "extracted_characters": 78203, "selected_characters": 78203, "extraction": "UTF-8 text", "selection_reason": "full extracted text fits allocation"}, {"path": "references/S-CHAT/fulltext.txt", "sha256": "7e5e268a64c716d3e87ebb5c11989e16afab9f10589a2190e281a0800a255287", "reading_scope": "full", "category": "source_original", "content": "\n===== docs/source/en/chat_templating.md =====\n<!--Copyright 2024 The HuggingFace Team. All rights reserved.\n\nLicensed under the Apache License, Version 2.0 (the \"License\"); you may not use this file except in compliance with\nthe License. You may obtain a copy of the License at\n\nhttp://www.apache.org/licenses/LICENSE-2.0\n\nUnless required by applicable law or agreed to in writing, software distributed under the License is distributed on\nan \"AS IS\" BASIS, WITHOUT WARRANTIES OR CONDITIONS OF ANY KIND, either express or implied. See the License for the\nspecific language governing permissions and limitations under the License.\n\n⚠️ Note that this file is in Markdown but contain specific syntax for our doc-builder (similar to MDX) that may not be\nrendered properly in your Markdown viewer.\n\n-->\n\n# Chat templates\n\nThe [chat basics](./conversations) guide covers how to store chat histories and generate text from chat models using [`TextGenerationPipeline`].\n\nThis guide is intended for more advanced users, and covers the underlying classes and methods, as well as the key concepts for understanding what's actually going on when you chat with a model.\n\nThe critical insight needed to understand chat models is this: All causal LMs, whether chat-trained or not, continue a sequence of tokens. When causal LMs are trained, the training usually begins with \"pre-training\" on a huge corpus of text, which creates a \"base\" model.\nThese base models are then often \"fine-tuned\" for chat, which means training them on data that is formatted as a sequence of messages. The chat is still just a sequence of tokens, though! The list of `role` and `content` dictionaries that you pass\nto a chat model get converted to a token sequence, often with control tokens like `<|user|>` or `<|assistant|>` or `<|end_of_message|>`, which allow the model to see the chat structure.\nThere are many possible chat formats, and different models may use different formats or control tokens, even if they were fine-tuned from the same base model!\n\nDon't panic, though - you don't need to memorize every possible chat format in order to use chat models. Chat models come with **chat templates**, which indicate how they expect chats to be formatted.\nYou can access these with the [`apply_chat_template`] method. Let's see two examples. Both of these models are fine-tuned from the same `Mistral-7B` base model:\n\n<hfoptions id=\"template\">\n<hfoption id=\"Mistral\">\n\n```py\nfrom transformers import AutoTokenizer\n\ntokenizer = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(\"mistralai/Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.1\")\nchat = [\n  {\"role\": \"user\", \"content\": \"Hello, how are you?\"},\n  {\"role\": \"assistant\", \"content\": \"I'm doing great. How can I help you today?\"},\n  {\"role\": \"user\", \"content\": \"I'd like to show off how chat templating works!\"},\n]\n\ntokenizer.apply_chat_template(chat, tokenize=False)\n```\n\n```md\n<s>[INST] Hello, how are you? [/INST]I'm doing great. How can I help you today?</s> [INST] I'd like to show off how chat templating works! [/INST]\n```\n\n</hfoption>\n<hfoption id=\"Zephyr\">\n\n```py\nfrom transformers import AutoTokenizer\n\ntokenizer = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(\"HuggingFaceH4/zephyr-7b-beta\")\nchat = [\n  {\"role\": \"user\", \"content\": \"Hello, how are you?\"},\n  {\"role\": \"assistant\", \"content\": \"I'm doing great. How can I help you today?\"},\n  {\"role\": \"user\", \"content\": \"I'd like to show off how chat templating works!\"},\n]\n\ntokenizer.apply_chat_template(chat, tokenize=False)\n```\n\n```md\n<|user|>\\nHello, how are you?</s>\\n<|assistant|>\\nI'm doing great. How can I help you today?</s>\\n<|user|>\\nI'd like to show off how chat templating works!</s>\\n\n```\n\n</hfoption>\n</hfoptions>\n\nMistral-7B-Instruct uses `[INST]` and `[/INST]` tokens to indicate the start and end of user messages, while Zephyr-7B uses `<|user|>` and `<|assistant|>` tokens to indicate speaker roles. This is why chat templates are important - with the wrong control tokens, these models would have drastically worse performance.\n\n## Using `apply_chat_template`\n\nThe input to `apply_chat_template` should be structured as a list of dictionaries with `role` and `content` keys. The `role` key specifies the speaker, and the `content` key contains the message. The common roles are:\n\n- `user` for messages from the user\n- `assistant` for messages from the model\n- `system` for directives on how the model should act (usually placed at the beginning of the chat)\n\n[`apply_chat_template`] takes this list and returns a formatted sequence. Set `tokenize=True` if you want to tokenize the sequence.\n\n```py\nimport torch\nfrom transformers import AutoModelForCausalLM, AutoTokenizer\n\ntokenizer = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(\"HuggingFaceH4/zephyr-7b-beta\")\nmodel = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(\"HuggingFaceH4/zephyr-7b-beta\", device_map=\"auto\", dtype=torch.bfloat16)\n\nmessages = [\n    {\"role\": \"system\", \"content\": \"You are a friendly chatbot who always responds in the style of a pirate\",},\n    {\"role\": \"user\", \"content\": \"How many helicopters can a human eat in one sitting?\"},\n ]\ntokenized_chat = tokenizer.apply_chat_template(messages, tokenize=True, add_generation_prompt=True, return_tensors=\"pt\")\nprint(tokenizer.decode(tokenized_chat[0]))\n```\n\n```md\n<|system|>\nYou are a friendly chatbot who always responds in the style of a pirate</s>\n<|user|>\nHow many helicopters can a human eat in one sitting?</s>\n<|assistant|>\n```\n\nPass the tokenized chat to [`~GenerationMixin.generate`] to generate a response.\n\n```py\noutputs = model.generate(tokenized_chat, max_new_tokens=128) \nprint(tokenizer.decode(outputs[0]))\n```\n\n```md\n<|system|>\nYou are a friendly chatbot who always responds in the style of a pirate</s>\n<|user|>\nHow many helicopters can a human eat in one sitting?</s>\n<|assistant|>\nMatey, I'm afraid I must inform ye that humans cannot eat helicopters. Helicopters are not food, they are flying machines. Food is meant to be eaten, like a hearty plate o' grog, a savory bowl o' stew, or a delicious loaf o' bread. But helicopters, they be for transportin' and movin' around, not for eatin'. So, I'd say none, me hearties. None at all.\n```\n\n> [!WARNING]\n> Some tokenizers add special `<bos>` and `<eos>` tokens. Chat templates should already include all the necessary special tokens, and adding additional special tokens is often incorrect or duplicated, hurting model performance. When you format text with `apply_chat_template(tokenize=False)`, make sure you set `add_special_tokens=False` if you tokenize later to avoid duplicating these tokens.\n> This isn't an issue if you use `apply_chat_template(tokenize=True)`, which means it's usually the safer option!\n\n### add_generation_prompt\n\nYou may have noticed the [add_generation_prompt](https://huggingface.co/docs/transformers/internal/tokenization_utils#transformers.PreTrainedTokenizerBase.apply_chat_template.add_generation_prompt) argument in the above examples.\nThis argument adds tokens to the end of the chat that indicate the start of an `assistant` response. Remember: Beneath all the chat abstractions, chat models are still just language models that continue a sequence of tokens!\nIf you include tokens that tell it that it's now in an `assistant` response, it will correctly write a response, but if you don't include these tokens, the model may get confused and do something strange, like **continuing** the user's message instead of replying to it!\n\nLet's see an example to understand what `add_generation_prompt` is actually doing. First, let's format a chat without `add_generation_prompt`:\n\n```py\ntokenized_chat = tokenizer.apply_chat_template(messages, tokenize=False, add_generation_prompt=False)\ntokenized_chat\n```\n\n```md\n<|im_start|>user\nHi there!<|im_end|>\n<|im_start|>assistant\nNice to meet you!<|im_end|>\n<|im_start|>user\nCan I ask a question?<|im_end|>\n```\n\nNow, let's format the same chat with `add_generation_prompt=True`:\n\n```py\ntokenized_chat = tokenizer.apply_chat_template(messages, tokenize=False, add_generation_prompt=True)\ntokenized_chat\n```\n\n```md\n<|im_start|>user\nHi there!<|im_end|>\n<|im_start|>assistant\nNice to meet you!<|im_end|>\n<|im_start|>user\nCan I ask a question?<|im_end|>\n<|im_start|>assistant\n\n```\n\nWhen `add_generation_prompt=True`, `<|im_start|>assistant` is added at the end to indicate the start of an `assistant` message. This lets the model know an `assistant` response is next.\n\nNot all models require generation prompts, and some models, like [Llama](./model_doc/llama), don't have any special tokens before the `assistant` response. In these cases, [add_generation_prompt](https://huggingface.co/docs/transformers/internal/tokenization_utils#transformers.PreTrainedTokenizerBase.apply_chat_template.add_generation_prompt) has no effect.\n\n### continue_final_message\n\nThe [continue_final_message](https://huggingface.co/docs/transformers/internal/tokenization_utils#transformers.PreTrainedTokenizerBase.apply_chat_template.continue_final_message) parameter controls whether the final message in the chat should be continued or not instead of starting a new one. It removes end of sequence tokens so that the model continues generation from the final message.\n\nThis is useful for “prefilling” a model response. In the example below, the model generates text that continues the JSON string rather than starting a new message. It can be very useful for improving the accuracy of instruction following when you know how to start its replies.\n\n```py\nchat = [\n    {\"role\": \"user\", \"content\": \"Can you format the answer in JSON?\"},\n    {\"role\": \"assistant\", \"content\": '{\"name\": \"'},\n]\n\nformatted_chat = tokenizer.apply_chat_template(chat, tokenize=True, return_dict=True, continue_final_message=True)\nmodel.generate(**formatted_chat)\n```\n\n> [!WARNING]\n> You shouldn't use [add_generation_prompt](https://huggingface.co/docs/transformers/internal/tokenization_utils#transformers.PreTrainedTokenizerBase.apply_chat_template.add_generation_prompt) and [continue_final_message](https://huggingface.co/docs/transformers/internal/tokenization_utils#transformers.PreTrainedTokenizerBase.apply_chat_template.continue_final_message) together. The former adds tokens that start a new message, while the latter removes end of sequence tokens. Using them together returns an error.\n\n[`TextGenerationPipeline`] sets [add_generation_prompt](https://huggingface.co/docs/transformers/internal/tokenization_utils#transformers.PreTrainedTokenizerBase.apply_chat_template.add_generation_prompt) to `True` by default to start a new message. However, if the final message in the chat has the `assistant` role, it assumes the message is a prefill and switches to `continue_final_message=True`. This is because most models don't support multiple consecutive assistant messages. To override this behavior, explicitly pass the [continue_final_message](https://huggingface.co/docs/transformers/internal/tokenization_utils#transformers.PreTrainedTokenizerBase.apply_chat_template.continue_final_message) argument to the pipeline.\n\n## Model training\n\nTraining a model with a chat template is a good way to ensure the template matches the tokens the model was trained on. Apply the chat template as a preprocessing step to your dataset. Set `add_generation_prompt=False` because the additional tokens to prompt an assistant response aren't helpful during training.\n\nAn example of preprocessing a dataset with a chat template is shown below.\n\n```py\nfrom transformers import AutoTokenizer\nfrom datasets import Dataset\n\ntokenizer = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(\"HuggingFaceH4/zephyr-7b-beta\")\n\nchat1 = [\n    {\"role\": \"user\", \"content\": \"Which is bigger, the moon or the sun?\"},\n    {\"role\": \"assistant\", \"content\": \"The sun.\"}\n]\nchat2 = [\n    {\"role\": \"user\", \"content\": \"Which is bigger, a virus or a bacterium?\"},\n    {\"role\": \"assistant\", \"content\": \"A bacterium.\"}\n]\n\ndataset = Dataset.from_dict({\"chat\": [chat1, chat2]})\ndataset = dataset.map(lambda x: {\"formatted_chat\": tokenizer.apply_chat_template(x[\"chat\"], tokenize=False, add_generation_prompt=False)})\nprint(dataset['formatted_chat'][0])\n```\n\n```md\n<|user|>\nWhich is bigger, the moon or the sun?</s>\n<|assistant|>\nThe sun.</s>\n```\n\nAfter this step, you can continue following the [training recipe](./tasks/language_modeling) for causal language models using the `formatted_chat` column.\n", "excerpt_ranges": [[0, 12274]], "range_unit": "zero-based half-open characters in extracted text", "extracted_characters": 12274, "selected_characters": 12274, "extraction": "UTF-8 text",
